    1: #!/usr/bin/env python3
    2: # -*- coding: utf-8 -*-
    3: """HTML card deck  ->  Slide IR.
    4: 
    5: STATUS: specification only. Every function below raises NotImplementedError.
    6: This is Phase 1 of the roadmap and it is deliberately the *first* thing built, because
    7: it exercises the whole IR -> renderer -> PPTX path with zero AI in the loop. If a
    8: 30-slide deck we already have cannot survive the round trip, no amount of prompt
    9: engineering will save the AI path.
   10: 
   11: Corpus (the fixtures this must handle -- all three verified, see foundation_research.md S3):
   12:     Slide Template.html                            4 cards
   13:     [REDACTED].html    17 cards
   14:     3D Logic 개발 테스트벤치 설계안_Ver2.html        9 cards
   15:                                                   -- 30 cards total
   16: 
   17: The convention, identical across all three
   18: [REDACTED]
   19:     <div class="hero"> <h1>DECK TITLE</h1> <p>subtitle</p> </div>   <- deck meta, NOT a slide
   20:     <section class="card" data-slide="N"> ... </section>            <- exactly one slide
   21:         <h2>          slide title       (exactly 1 per card, all 30 verified)
   22:         <p>           lead paragraph
   23:         <h3>          sub-heading
   24:         <ul><li>      bullets
   25:         <table>       table
   26:         <div class="alert-box"|"warn-box"|"ok-box">   callout, 3 variants
   27:         <div class="mermaid">                          diagram
   28:         <pre><code>   code block
   29:         <div class="tabs">   tabbed code -> ONE `code` block with multiple variants
   30:         <img src="data:image/jpeg;base64,...">         image (jpeg, not png)
   31:         <span class="step-badge">  numbered step
   32:         <div class="mini-card"> in .grid-2/.grid-3     peer cards
   33:         <code>        inline code -- 111 occurrences, MUST become Run(code=True)
   34: 
   35: `data-slide` is 0-based and, in this corpus, always equals the card's document order.
   36: Do NOT trust it as an ordering key: order by document position and use `data-slide`
   37: only to build the slide `id`. (If they ever disagree, that is a bug in the source deck
   38: and the adapter should warn, not silently reorder.)
   39: 
   40: Ignore entirely (chrome, not content)
   41: [REDACTED]
   42:     .mode-toggle  .slide-nav  .page-info  .container  <script>  <style>
   43:     .sim-control-panel  .sim-slider  .sim-control-group
   44: 
   45: The `sim-*` family is an interactive simulator in the 3D Logic deck. It has no static
   46: representation at all. Dropping it silently would quietly lose a slide's entire point,
   47: so :func:`parse_deck` MUST emit a LOSSY warning for it (see `ConversionReport`), and the
   48: slide should fall back to its `<h3>`/`<p>` prose.
   49: 
   50: Dependencies: BeautifulSoup4 + lxml. Do NOT use a regex; the corpus nests
   51: (`<img>` lives *inside* `.photo-desc` inside `.photo-placeholder` inside `.photo-grid`).
   52: """
   53: 
   54: from __future__ import annotations
   55: 
   56: from dataclasses import dataclass, field
   57: from pathlib import Path
   58: from typing import Any
   59: 
   60: from ..ir.models import Block, Deck
   61: 
   62: 
   63: @dataclass
   64: class ConversionReport:
   65:     """What the adapter could not faithfully carry across.
   66: 
   67:     Exists because the failure mode we care about is *silent* loss. A slide that renders
   68:     beautifully but has quietly dropped its only diagram is worse than a loud failure.
   69:     ``lossy`` is printed by the CLI and asserted-empty by the round-trip test.
   70:     """
   71: 
   72:     slides_in: int = 0
   73:     slides_out: int = 0
   74:     lossy: list[str] = field(default_factory=list)      # "slide 6: .sim-control-panel dropped"
   75:     unmapped_classes: set[str] = field(default_factory=set)
   76: 
   77:     def warn(self, slide_idx: int, message: str) -> None:
   78:         self.lossy.append(f"slide {slide_idx}: {message}")
   79: 
   80: 
   81: def parse_deck(html_path: Path, *, strict: bool = False) -> tuple[Deck, ConversionReport]:
   82:     """Parse one HTML card deck into a validated :class:`Deck`.
   83: 
   84:     Args:
   85:         html_path: the .html file. Read with ``encoding='utf-8'`` -- the corpus is
   86:             Korean and cp949 will mangle it.
   87:         strict: if True, raise on the first unmapped construct instead of degrading.
   88:             The round-trip test runs with ``strict=False`` and asserts the report is
   89:             clean, which gives a better error message than an exception would.
   90: 
   91:     Returns:
   92:         ``(deck, report)``. ``deck`` is schema-valid IR. ``report`` lists everything
   93:         that was dropped or degraded.
   94: 
   95:     Raises:
   96:         FileNotFoundError, IRValidationError; and any construct error when ``strict``.
   97: 
   98:     Invariants:
   99:         * ``len(deck.slides) == report.slides_in == number of section.card elements``
  100:           -- the adapter never invents or merges slides. One card in, one slide out.
  101:         * Every ``slide.title`` comes from that card's single ``<h2>``; a card with zero
  102:           or two ``<h2>`` elements is a hard error (never seen in the corpus, so if it
  103:           fires, the source deck broke its own convention).
  104:         * ``deck.deck.title`` comes from ``.hero h1``, NOT from any card.
  105:         * No block is ever emitted with empty text.
  106:     """
  107:     raise NotImplementedError
  108: 
  109: 
  110: def choose_layout(blocks: list[Block], manifest: dict[str, Any]) -> str:
  111:     """Pick the template layout that best fits a slide's content. The crux of the adapter.
  112: 
  113:     The HTML has no notion of a PowerPoint layout, so this is where the mapping is
  114:     *invented* -- and it is the single place where a bad decision produces an ugly deck.
  115:     Rule-based, in priority order (first match wins):
  116: 
  117:         image blocks present            -> a 'picture' role layout
  118:         table is the dominant block     -> 'table' role, else 'bullets' (a table is
  119:                                            just a wide body placeholder)
  120:         diagram present                 -> 'picture' role (we place a rendered/native
  121:                                            diagram into the picture box)
  122:         cards / steps present           -> 'two-content' or 'multi-content' by item count
  123:         bullets + heading only          -> 'bullets'
  124:         lead paragraph only, no bullets -> 'title-only'  (a statement slide)
  125:         nothing but the title           -> 'section-divider'
  126: 
  127:     Args:
  128:         blocks: the slide's parsed blocks, in order.
  129:         manifest: the parsed ``reference/layout_manifest.json``. ``semantic_role`` on each
  130:             layout is what this function matches against -- never a hard-coded layout name,
  131:             because the template can be re-authored and the names will change.
  132: 
  133:     Returns:
  134:         A ``layout_id`` guaranteed to exist in ``manifest``.
  135: 
  136:     Raises:
  137:         LookupError: if the manifest has no layout for a required role. This is a real
  138:             possibility (a minimal template may have no 'picture' layout) and the caller
  139:             should fall back to 'bullets' + a free-floating picture rather than crash.
  140:     """
  141:     raise NotImplementedError
  142: 
  143: 
  144: def parse_rich_text(el: Any) -> Any:
  145:     """One inline HTML element -> :class:`~aippt.ir.models.RichText`.
  146: 
  147:     Walks the element's children and maps: ``<strong>/<b>`` -> ``b``, ``<em>/<i>`` -> ``i``,
  148:     ``<code>`` -> ``code``, ``<a href>`` -> ``link``. Everything else contributes its text
  149:     with no styling. Whitespace is collapsed (the source is pretty-printed HTML, so raw
  150:     ``.text`` is full of newlines and indentation).
  151: 
  152:     Getting ``<code>`` right is the whole point: it is the most common inline mark in the
  153:     corpus by an order of magnitude, and a deck of API documentation that renders
  154:     ``VEO_Focus_Initialize()`` in body prose instead of monospace looks broken.
  155:     """
  156:     raise NotImplementedError
  157: 
  158: 
  159: def extract_image(img_el: Any, report: ConversionReport, slide_idx: int) -> Any:
  160:     """``<img src="data:image/jpeg;base64,...">`` -> an ``image`` block.
  161: 
  162:     The data URI is kept *verbatim* in the IR (``source.kind == "data_uri"``). The IR
  163:     stays a single self-contained JSON file -- no sidecar asset directory to keep in sync,
  164:     which matters because the IR gets passed to an LLM and round-tripped through a QA loop.
  165:     Decoding happens once, late, in ``render/renderer.py``.
  166: 
  167:     A 619 KB HTML file is ~600 KB of base64. The resulting IR is big but still text.
  168:     If that ever becomes a problem, switch ``kind`` to ``"path"`` and spill to disk --
  169:     the schema already supports it and nothing else needs to change.
  170: 
  171:     Malformed / truncated base64 must be reported as lossy, never allowed to raise here:
  172:     a broken image should cost one picture, not the whole deck.
  173:     """
  174:     raise NotImplementedError

    1: #!/usr/bin/env python3
    2: # -*- coding: utf-8 -*-
    3: """LLM  ->  Slide IR.  Model-agnostic by construction.
    4: 
    5: STATUS: specification only. Every function below raises NotImplementedError.
    6: 
    7: The single invariant this module exists to protect
    8: [REDACTED]
    9: **The model never emits PPTX, OOXML, DrawingML, coordinates, fonts or colours.**
   10: It emits schema-valid IR: a ``layout_id`` plus placeholder content. Everything visual
   11: comes from the template and the renderer. If you ever find yourself adding an `x`, `y`,
   12: `rgb` or `font_size` field to the IR to make a slide look right, the bug is in the
   13: renderer or the template -- fix it there.
   14: 
   15: Why structured output and not free-text-then-parse
   16: [REDACTED]
   17: "Return JSON" in a system prompt yields JSON *most* of the time, and the failures are
   18: the expensive kind: a stray markdown fence, a trailing comma, a hallucinated field. Every
   19: supported provider can constrain output to a schema, so we use that and get a hard
   20: guarantee instead of a soft one:
   21: 
   22:     Claude  -> tool use / `tools=[{input_schema: SCHEMA}]`, force with tool_choice
   23:     OpenAI  -> Structured Outputs / `response_format={type: json_schema, strict: true}`
   24:     Gemini  -> `response_mime_type="application/json"` + `response_schema`
   25: 
   26: All three consume the *same* ``ir/schema.json``. That is the entire point of having an IR:
   27: the schema is the neutral contract, and the model behind it is a swappable implementation
   28: detail. Adding a provider must not require touching the renderer, the prompts, or the IR.
   29: 
   30: Two-stage generation (see prompts/)
   31: [REDACTED]
   32:     stage 1  10_outline_storyboard.md  topic + sources -> deck narrative, one line/slide
   33:     stage 2  20_slide_fill.md          one slide's outline entry -> that slide's IR
   34: 
   35: Stage 2 runs per-slide and is parallelisable. The split exists because a single call that
   36: has to hold the whole deck's narrative *and* respect 30 different per-placeholder
   37: character budgets does neither job well: it drifts on structure and overflows on length.
   38: Separating "what story do we tell" from "what words go in this box" fixes both.
   39: """

   84: def build_system_prompt(manifest: dict[str, Any], catalog_md: str) -> str:

   86: 
   87:     The catalog (``reference/layout_catalog.md``) is injected *whole*. It carries the two
   88:     things the model cannot guess and must not invent:
   89: 
   90:       * the exact set of legal ``layout_id`` values, and

   92:         Korean chars").

   95:     to a length that reads well in chat and overflows a 4-inch box every time -- and since
   96:     python-pptx cannot autofit, that overflow is permanent and invisible until render.
   97: 
   98:     Note the budget is stated in BOTH Latin and Korean character counts, because a Hangul
   99:     glyph is roughly twice the advance width of a Latin one. A model told "60 characters"
  100:     with no script qualifier will happily overflow a Korean slide by 2x.

  139: 
  140:     The loop, and why it is bounded:

  142:         1. outline  -> validate every ``layout_id`` against the manifest
  143:         2. per slide: fill -> validate against ``ir/schema.json`` -> check the budget
  144:         3. on failure, re-prompt ONCE per slide with the *specific* error appended
  145:            ("slides[3].blocks[1].rows[2]: row has 3 cells but the table declares 4 columns").
  146:            A precise, machine-generated error message repairs on the first retry the
  147:            overwhelming majority of the time. A vague one ("invalid JSON") does not.
  148:         4. after ``max_repairs``, FAIL LOUD.
  149: 
  150:     Never silently drop a bad slide and never substitute a default. A 11-slide deck that
  151:     was asked for 12 is a defect the user cannot see; an exception is one they can.
  152: 
  153:     Budget violations are *repairable*, not fatal: the retry says "this is 40 chars over,
  154:     cut it". Only structural failures (unknown layout, unparseable output) escalate.
  155: 
  156:     Raises:
  157:         RuntimeError: repairs exhausted. The message names the slide and the reason.
  158:     """

    1: #!/usr/bin/env python3
    2: # -*- coding: utf-8 -*-
    3: """Slide IR  ->  .pptx, built on the template's own master.
    4: 
    5: STATUS: specification only. Every function below raises NotImplementedError.
    6: 
    7: The rule that makes this whole approach work
    8: [REDACTED]
    9:     prs = Presentation(TEMPLATE_PATH)          # open the REAL template
   10:     layout = prs.slide_layouts[...]            # use ITS layouts
   11:     slide = prs.slides.add_slide(layout)       # inherit master, theme, fonts, brand
   12: 
   13: We never construct ``Presentation()`` empty and restyle it. A blank Presentation carries
   14: python-pptx's stock 4:3 Office theme; every colour and font would have to be re-applied by
   15: hand, the corporate master would be gone, and the output would be un-editable by a designer
   16: in any meaningful way. Opening the template is not an optimisation, it is the design.
   17: 
   18: Corollary: the template ships with N existing slides. ``add_slide`` appends after them, so
   19: the renderer must decide -- and document -- whether it strips them first. Default: strip,
   20: via :func:`clear_existing_slides`, because those slides are the template's *examples*, not
   21: our content.
   22: 
   23: python-pptx gotchas this module has to absorb (details in foundation_research.md S4)
   24: [REDACTED]
   25: 1. **No autofit.** ``text_frame.auto_size`` and ``word_wrap`` set the XML flags, but the
   26:    actual shrink-to-fit is computed by PowerPoint's layout engine at open time -- which
   27:    python-pptx does not have. Text overflows silently. Hence :mod:`aippt.render.fit`.
   28: 2. **Unused placeholders still render.** A layout placeholder that is never filled shows
   29:    its prompt text ("Click to add text") in the *editor*; empty ones can also leave stray
   30:    bullets. Any placeholder not used by a slide MUST be explicitly removed:
   31:    ``ph._element.getparent().remove(ph._element)``.
   32: 3. **No SmartArt.** There is no API and no plausible way to synthesise one. See diagrams.py.
   33: 4. **No native chart-from-image.** Charts must be built via ``add_chart`` with real data,
   34:    which the IR does not currently carry. Out of scope for Phase 1.
   35: 5. **Placeholder geometry may be inherited** (``ph.left`` is None on the layout). The
   36:    manifest already resolves this against the master -- read the manifest, do not re-derive.
   37: """

   97:     and PowerPoint will report the file as corrupt. Both halves, in that order.

  154: def place_block(slide: Any, block: Block, target: Any, budget: dict[str, Any],
  155:                 report: RenderReport) -> None:
  156:     """Render one block into a target placeholder (or a free-floating shape).
  157: 
  158:     Dispatch:
  159:         heading/paragraph/bullets -> paragraphs in the body placeholder, ``para.level``
  160:                                      carrying the bullet level
  161:         callout                   -> rounded rectangle, left accent bar; fill colour by
  162:                                      variant (info/warn/ok)
  163:         table                     -> ``shapes.add_table``; a TABLE placeholder cannot be
  164:                                      filled directly, so add the table at the placeholder's
  165:                                      geometry and then delete the placeholder
  166:         code                      -> monospace textbox on a dark fill. Multi-variant
  167:                                      (the tabbed C++/C# case): PowerPoint has no tabs, so
  168:                                      lay the variants side by side if 2 and they fit,
  169:                                      otherwise emit consecutive slides

  171:         diagram                   -> delegate to :mod:`aippt.render.diagrams`
  172:         steps                     -> numbered chevrons, left to right
  173:         cards                     -> N rounded rectangles across the placeholder width
  174: 
  175:     ``budget`` is this placeholder's entry from the manifest. Check the block against it
  176:     BEFORE writing, and record an overflow warning rather than truncating silently.
  177:     """
  178:     raise NotImplementedError
  179: 
  180: 
  181: def place_image(slide: Any, image_block: Any, target_box: tuple[int, int, int, int]) -> Any:
  182:     """base64 data-URI -> ``add_picture``, aspect-fit inside ``target_box``.
  183: 
  184:     The pipeline (all three corpus images are ``image/jpeg``, not png)::
  185: 
  186:         header, b64 = data_uri.split(",", 1)          # 'data:image/jpeg;base64'
  187:         blob = base64.b64decode(b64)
  188:         stream = io.BytesIO(blob)                      # add_picture accepts a file-like
  189:         pic = slide.shapes.add_picture(stream, left, top, width=..., height=...)
  190: 
  191:     Aspect-fit, not stretch. Passing BOTH width and height to ``add_picture`` distorts the
  192:     image; pass ONE and let python-pptx derive the other from the intrinsic size, then
  193:     centre it in the box::
  194: 
  195:         box_ar = box_w / box_h
  196:         img_ar = pic.image.size[0] / pic.image.size[1]
  197:         if img_ar > box_ar:  scale to width   else:  scale to height
  198: 
  199:     ``pic.image.size`` needs Pillow. Without it python-pptx can still place the picture but
  200:     cannot read its intrinsic dimensions, so aspect-fit silently becomes stretch -- which
  201:     is why Pillow is a hard dependency, not an optional one.
  202: 
  203:     Returns the created picture shape.
  204:     """
  205:     raise NotImplementedError

  216:     stay. Everything else, if empty, goes -- an unfilled body placeholder shows "Click to

  219:     Returns: number removed.
  220:     """
  221:     raise NotImplementedError

    1: #!/usr/bin/env python3
    2: # -*- coding: utf-8 -*-
    3: """Text fitting and overflow control. **The #1 design risk of this project.**
    4: 
    5: STATUS: specification only. Every function below raises NotImplementedError.
    6: 
    7: Why this module is the hard one
    8: -------------------------------
    9: PowerPoint's "shrink text on overflow" is not stored as a result, it is stored as an
   10: *instruction* (``<a:normAutofit fontScale="..."/>``) that PowerPoint's layout engine
   11: evaluates when it opens the file. python-pptx writes the instruction but cannot evaluate
   12: it: it has no font metrics, no line breaker, no layout engine. So:
   13: 
   14:     text_frame.word_wrap = True
   15:     text_frame.auto_size = MSO_AUTO_SIZE.TEXT_TO_FIT_SHAPE     # <- does NOT fit the text
   16: 
   17: Overflowing text does not error, does not warn, and does not visibly clip in the XML. It
   18: simply renders off the bottom of the box. **Every deck this project generates is one
   19: verbose LLM sentence away from a broken slide**, and nothing in the stack will tell you.
   20: 
   21: The three-layer defence
   22: -----------------------
   23: Layer 1 -- PREVENT (the only layer that actually scales).
   24:     Inject the per-placeholder content budget into the author prompt. A model told
   25:     "max 6 bullets x 9 words, title <= 42 Korean characters" mostly complies. This is
   26:     cheap, it is upstream of everything, and it removes the problem rather than managing
   27:     it. ``analyze_pptx_master.py`` computes these budgets; that is why it exists.
   28: 
   29: Layer 2 -- MEASURE + DEGRADE (this module).
   30:     Before writing, estimate the rendered height. If it exceeds the box, degrade in a
   31:     fixed, documented order (:func:`fit_text_frame`). Deterministic, no LLM in the loop.
   32: 
   33: Layer 3 -- VERIFY (render -> PNG -> check).
   34:     Layers 1 and 2 both rest on an *estimate*. The only ground truth is a render.
   35:     LibreOffice converts the .pptx to PNG; :func:`detect_overflow_in_png` checks whether
   36:     ink appears where it should not. This is the authoritative check and the only one
   37:     that catches a bad font-metric assumption.
   38: 
   39: A note on honesty: :func:`estimate_text_height` is a model, not a measurement. It will be
   40: wrong sometimes. That is precisely why layer 3 exists and why the degradation ladder is
   41: ordered to fail *safe* (smaller, then fewer) rather than to fail *pretty*.

   52: #: and the whole prevent-layer silently stops working. Import them; do not re-type them.
   53: from ..analyze_pptx_master import (  # noqa: F401

   56:     LATIN_CHAR_WIDTH_RATIO,
   57:     LINE_HEIGHT_RATIO,
   58: )

   60: #: Never shrink below this: an 11pt bullet on a projected slide is unreadable from the
   61: #: back of a room, and a legible slide with less content beats a full slide nobody can read.
   62: MIN_BODY_PT = 12.0
   63: MIN_TITLE_PT = 20.0

   71: class FitResult:

   80: def is_cjk(ch: str) -> bool:
   81:     """True for Hangul / Han / Kana -- the glyphs that are full-width.
   82: 
   83:     Ranges: Hangul syllables U+AC00-D7AF, Hangul Jamo U+1100-11FF, CJK Unified
   84:     U+4E00-9FFF, CJK Ext-A U+3400-4DBF, Kana U+3040-30FF, CJK punctuation U+3000-303F.
   85: 
   86:     This matters more than it looks: the corpus is Korean, and a width model that assumes
   87:     Latin metrics underestimates a Korean line by ~2x. That single mistake would make
   88:     every budget in the catalog wrong in the dangerous direction.
   89:     """
   90:     raise NotImplementedError
   91: 
   92: 
   93: def visual_width(text: str, font_pt: float) -> float:
   94:     """Rendered width of ``text`` in points, mixing scripts correctly.
   95: 
   96:         sum(font_pt * (CJK_CHAR_WIDTH_RATIO if is_cjk(c) else LATIN_CHAR_WIDTH_RATIO)
   97:             for c in text)
   98: 
   99:     A per-character sum, not ``len(text) * ratio``: the corpus routinely mixes
  100:     ("VEO_Focus_SetPosition() 호출 시 모터가 이동합니다") and a single ratio for such a
  101:     string is wrong for both halves.
  102: 
  103:     Bold text is ~5% wider; inline ``code`` is monospace and typically *narrower* per
  104:     char than proportional Latin. Both are inside the SAFETY margin -- do not model them
  105:     until a real render says you must.
  106:     """
  107:     raise NotImplementedError
  108: 
  109:

  113: 
  114:     Wrap on spaces for Latin. **Korean wraps at any character** -- there are no spaces
  115:     inside a Hangul clause -- so a Latin-style word-wrap model badly overestimates the
  116:     line count for Korean text. Break CJK runs per character.
  117: 
  118:     Returns ``(height_pt, line_count)``.
  119:     """
  120:     raise NotImplementedError
  121: 
  122:

  125:

  128:     will overflow.
  129: 
  130:     Cheap and pure. Call it in the AI adapter's validation step so a budget violation is
  131:     caught while a *retry* is still possible, not after the deck is built.
  132:     """
  133:     raise NotImplementedError
  134: 
  135: 
  136: def fit_text_frame(text_frame: Any, blocks: list[Any], box_emu: tuple[int, int],
  137:                    base_pt: float, *, min_pt: float = MIN_BODY_PT) -> FitResult:
  138:     """Make content fit a box. The degradation ladder, in strict order.
  139: 
  140:     1. **Fits as-is** -> write it. Done.
  141:     2. **Shrink** -- reduce font size in ``SHRINK_STEP_PT`` steps, at most
  142:        ``MAX_SHRINK_STEPS``, never below ``min_pt``. Also set ``<a:normAutofit
  143:        fontScale>`` so that PowerPoint agrees with us when a human later edits the text.
  144:     3. **Tighten** -- reduce ``space_before``/``space_after`` and line spacing to 0.9.
  145:        Buys roughly one line. Visually cheap.
  146:     4. **Split** -- move the overflow onto a continuation slide using the same layout,
  147:        title suffixed " (cont.)". This is the *correct* answer for genuinely long content
  148:        and the one a human would choose.
  149:     5. **Truncate** -- last resort, and it is a defect: record it in ``RenderReport.dropped``
  150:        and let the caller decide whether to fail the build. Never truncate quietly.
  151: 
  152:     Deliberately NOT in the ladder: rewriting the text with an LLM to make it shorter.
  153:     That is layer 1's job, upstream, where the model still has the source material and the
  154:     intent. Doing it here -- with only the rendered string in hand -- produces a summary
  155:     of a summary, and the result reads like it.
  156: 
  157:     Returns the :class:`FitResult` describing what was actually done.
  158:     """
  159:     raise NotImplementedError
  160: 
  161: 
  162: def split_overflow(blocks: list[Any], box_emu: tuple[int, int], font_pt: float) -> tuple[list[Any], list[Any]]:
  163:     """Split blocks into ``(fits_on_this_slide, goes_to_continuation)``.
  164: 
  165:     Split at block boundaries where possible; split *within* a bullets block only if a
  166:     single bullets block alone overflows. Never split a table's header from its body, and
  167:     never orphan a heading from the first bullet under it -- both look like a rendering
  168:     bug rather than a deliberate break.
  169:     """
  170:     raise NotImplementedError
  171: 
  172:

  185:       * overlapping ink bboxes from two different placeholders -> collision.

    1: #!/usr/bin/env python3
    2: # -*- coding: utf-8 -*-
    3: """Mermaid-ish diagram  ->  something a PowerPoint slide can show.
    4: 
    5: STATUS: specification only. Every function below raises NotImplementedError.
    6: 
    7: The corpus: 3 ``.mermaid`` blocks (1 ``sequenceDiagram``, 2 flowcharts) plus 1 hand-drawn
    8: inline ``<svg>``. Small, but diagrams carry the highest information density per slide in
    9: the whole deck -- these are architecture decks, and the architecture *is* the diagram.
   10: 
   11: Why not SmartArt
   12: ----------------
   13: python-pptx cannot create SmartArt. There is no API, and the underlying ``diagramData``
   14: part is a dense, undocumented, version-sensitive format that PowerPoint regenerates on
   15: open. Hand-writing it is not a shortcut, it is a maintenance liability with a hard ceiling.
   16: Ruled out, permanently.
   17: 
   18: The hybrid (recommended -- see foundation_research.md S7)
   19: [REDACTED]
   20: Neither pure strategy is right, because the two failure modes are different:
   21: 
   22:     NATIVE autoshapes      editable, on-brand, vector-crisp, no external dependency
   23:                            -- but only tractable for *simple* graphs. A general graph
   24:                            layout engine is a project, not a function.
   25: 
   26:     RASTERISED (mermaid-cli -> PNG -> add_picture)
   27:                            handles arbitrary mermaid, looks exactly like the HTML deck
   28:                            -- but produces a flat image: not editable, not on-brand, and
   29:                            it goes fuzzy the moment anyone resizes it.
   30: 
   31: So: **decide per diagram, by complexity.** ``choose_strategy`` draws the line. Simple
   32: linear flowcharts -- which is what most of the corpus actually contains -- render native
   33: and stay editable. Anything genuinely complex rasterises, which is honest: an unreadable
   34: native approximation of a 20-node graph is worse than a clean picture of it.
   35: 
   36: An escape hatch matters here. The IR's ``render_hint`` lets an author force either path,
   37: because the person who wrote the diagram knows things the node count does not.
   38: """
   39: 
   40: from __future__ import annotations
   41: 
   42: from dataclasses import dataclass
   43: from pathlib import Path
   44: from typing import Any, Literal
   45: 
   46: Strategy = Literal["native", "image"]
   47: 
   48: #: Above this many nodes, a native layout stops being legible and starts being a liability.
   49: #: Tuned from the corpus, not from theory: its flowcharts are 4-7 nodes and lay out cleanly;
   50: #: its sequence diagram is 3 participants x 5 messages. Revisit with real decks.
   51: NATIVE_NODE_LIMIT = 8
   52: NATIVE_EDGE_LIMIT = 10
   53: 
   54: 
   55: @dataclass
   56: class Node:
   57:     id: str
   58:     label: str
   59:     shape: str = "rect"          # rect | round | diamond (mermaid [] / () / {})
   60: 
   61: 
   62: @dataclass
   63: class Edge:
   64:     src: str
   65:     dst: str
   66:     label: str | None = None
   67:     style: str = "solid"         # solid (-->) | dashed (-.->)
   68: 
   69: 
   70: @dataclass
   71: class Graph:
   72:     nodes: list[Node]
   73:     edges: list[Edge]
   74:     direction: str = "TD"        # TD | LR -- mermaid's `graph LR` / `flowchart TD`
   75:     kind: str = "flowchart"      # flowchart | sequence | other
   76: 
   77: 
   78: def parse_mermaid(source: str) -> Graph:
   79:     """Parse the mermaid subset the corpus actually uses. Not a general mermaid parser.
   80: 
   81:     Supported, because it is what the 30 slides contain::
   82: 
   83:         flowchart TD / graph LR
   84:             A[Rectangle label]  B(Rounded)  C{Diamond}
   85:             A --> B             A -.-> C          A -->|edge label| B
   86: 
   87:         sequenceDiagram
   88:             participant App as China Client App
   89:             App->>SDK: VEO_Focus_Initialize()
   90:             HW-->>App: Direct Event Callback
   91: 
   92:     Anything outside that subset must raise :class:`UnsupportedDiagram`, NOT be
   93:     half-parsed. A partially-understood diagram renders as a *wrong* diagram, which is
   94:     strictly worse than an honest fallback to the image path -- a wrong architecture
   95:     diagram in an engineering deck is a real cost.
   96: 
   97:     Raises:
   98:         UnsupportedDiagram: with the offending line, so the caller can fall back cleanly.
   99:     """
  100:     raise NotImplementedError
  101: 
  102: 
  103: class UnsupportedDiagram(ValueError):
  104:     """This mermaid source is outside the native parser's subset. Rasterise it instead."""
  105: 
  106: 
  107: def choose_strategy(graph: Graph | None, source: str, hint: str = "auto") -> Strategy:
  108:     """Native shapes, or a rendered picture?
  109: 
  110:     ``hint`` of ``"native"`` / ``"image"`` is honoured verbatim -- the author overrides us.
  111: 
  112:     On ``"auto"``:
  113:         * parse failed (``graph is None``)              -> image
  114:         * ``kind == "sequence"``                        -> native (a sequence diagram is
  115:           just lifelines and arrows on a grid -- genuinely easy, and it is 1 of our 3)
  116:         * nodes > NATIVE_NODE_LIMIT or edges > NATIVE_EDGE_LIMIT -> image
  117:         * cyclic graph                                  -> image (layered layout below
  118:           assumes a DAG and will loop forever on a cycle)
  119:         * otherwise                                     -> native
  120:     """
  121:     raise NotImplementedError
  122: 
  123: 
  124: def render_native(slide: Any, graph: Graph, box_emu: tuple[int, int, int, int]) -> list[Any]:
  125:     """Draw the graph as real PowerPoint autoshapes. Editable, on-brand, vector.
  126: 
  127:     Layout: layered (Sugiyama-lite), which is all a DAG this small needs.
  128:         1. longest-path layering -> assign each node a rank (depth from a root);
  129:         2. order nodes within a rank to reduce crossings (one median pass is enough at
  130:            this size; do not build a proper crossing minimiser);
  131:         3. position: rank -> the ``direction`` axis, index-within-rank -> the other axis;
  132:         4. scale the whole thing to fit ``box_emu``, preserving aspect;
  133:         5. nodes via ``shapes.add_shape(MSO_SHAPE.ROUNDED_RECTANGLE, ...)``,
  134:            edges via ``shapes.add_connector(MSO_CONNECTOR.ELBOW, ...)``.
  135: 
  136:     Style from the THEME, never hard-coded: node fill = the theme slot the manifest
  137:     identified as carrying the brand accent, text = the master's minor font. A native
  138:     diagram whose red is a hand-typed ``#DB0032`` will drift the day the brand changes.
  139: 
  140:     Connectors: ``add_connector`` returns a free-floating line. Bind it to the shapes with
  141:     ``connector.begin_connect(shape, cxn_idx)`` / ``end_connect`` so it stays attached when
  142:     a human drags a box -- that reattachment is most of the value of going native at all.
  143: 
  144:     Returns the created shapes.
  145:     """
  146:     raise NotImplementedError
  147: 
  148: 
  149: def render_image(source: str, out_dir: Path, *, scale: int = 3) -> Path:
  150:     """Rasterise mermaid via mermaid-cli. The fallback path.
  151: 
  152:         mmdc -i diagram.mmd -o diagram.png -b transparent -s 3
  153: 
  154:     ``-s 3`` (3x) because a 1x PNG on a 13.3in slide is visibly soft on a projector.
  155:     Transparent background so the master's slide fill shows through -- a white box on a
  156:     non-white master is an instant tell.
  157: 
  158:     Requires Node + ``@mermaid-js/mermaid-cli``. That is an external, non-Python dependency
  159:     and it WILL be missing on some machine: check for it once at startup and degrade
  160:     loudly (a text-only slide plus a clear warning), never crash mid-deck.
  161: 
  162:     Returns the PNG path.
  163: 
  164:     Raises:
  165:         FileNotFoundError: mmdc is not on PATH. Message must say how to install it.
  166:     """
  167:     raise NotImplementedError
  168: 
  169: 
  170: def render_diagram(slide: Any, diagram_block: Any, box_emu: tuple[int, int, int, int],
  171:                    work_dir: Path) -> str:
  172:     """Entry point: IR ``diagram`` block -> shapes on the slide. Returns the strategy used.
  173: 
  174:     Orchestrates: parse (tolerating failure) -> :func:`choose_strategy` -> render.
  175:     If the native path raises for any reason, fall back to the image path rather than

  177:     """
  178:     raise NotImplementedError

    1: # System Prompt — Deck Author
    2: 
    3: > Loaded as the `system` message for **both** generation stages. Provider-agnostic:
    4: > the same text is used for Claude, Codex/OpenAI and Gemini. Injection points are
    5: > `{{DOUBLE_BRACE}}`; `ai_adapter.build_system_prompt()` fills them.
    6: >
    7: > Mark this prompt as cacheable (`cache_control: ephemeral` on Claude) — it is identical
    8: > for every slide in a deck, and the layout catalog makes it the largest single block of
    9: > tokens in the run.
   10: 
   11: ---
   12: 
   13: You are a senior technical presentation author at Vieworks, an industrial imaging company.
   14: You write engineering decks for R&D, field-application engineers, and customer teams —
   15: audiences who are technical, time-poor, and allergic to filler.
   16: 
   17: ## The one rule that governs everything you do
   18: 
   19: **You produce structured content. You never produce design.**
   20: 
   21: You emit JSON conforming to the Slide IR schema: a `layout_id` and the content that goes
   22: in it. You do **not** emit — and must never attempt to emit — coordinates, sizes, fonts,
   23: colours, hex values, XML, OOXML, DrawingML, or any styling instruction of any kind.
   24: 
   25: Every visual decision is already made, by a corporate PowerPoint template you cannot see
   26: and do not need to see. A deterministic renderer places your content into that template's
   27: own layouts. If you specify a position, you are not helping — you are corrupting a design
   28: system that a human designer owns.
   29: 
   30: There is exactly one exception, and it is not styling: a `diagram` block carries **Mermaid
   31: source**. Mermaid is a text notation for graph *structure*, not geometry. Write the nodes
   32: and edges; the renderer decides how they are drawn.
   33: 
   34: ## Content budgets — this is not advice
   35: 
   36: Each layout's placeholders have a hard character budget, given to you below. The rendering
   37: engine **cannot shrink text to fit**. There is no autofit. Text that exceeds its box does
   38: not wrap gracefully, does not shrink, and does not raise an error — it renders off the
   39: bottom edge of the slide and the deck ships broken.
   40: 
   41: Therefore:
   42: 
   43: - Treat every budget as a hard ceiling, and **aim for 80% of it.** Bold text, inline code
   44:   and punctuation all consume more width than the estimate assumes.
   45: - Korean and English have **different** budgets. A Hangul glyph is about twice the width of
   46:   a Latin one. Where both are given, and your text mixes scripts at all, use the **Korean**
   47:   figure.
   48: - If the content genuinely does not fit, **split it across two slides.** Do not compress it
   49:   into an unreadable dense paragraph. Two clear slides beat one overstuffed one, always.
   50: 
   51: ## How to write
   52: 
   53: - **One idea per slide.** If a slide needs "and", it is probably two slides.
   54: - **Titles are claims, not labels.** "Trigger latency drops 40% with the direct callback"
   55:   earns its place; "Architecture Overview" does not.
   56: - Lead with a single framing sentence, then the evidence.
   57: - Bullets are **fragments, not sentences.** No terminal periods. Parallel grammatical form.
   58: - Use inline `code` for every API name, type, file, flag and identifier. It is the most
   59:   common inline mark in this house style and its absence reads as sloppiness.
   60: - Prefer a `table` over prose whenever you are comparing more than two things on more than
   61:   one axis.
   62: - Use a `callout` for the one thing the audience must remember — at most one per slide, and
   63:   not on every slide.
   64: - Never invent facts, API names, numbers or behaviour. If the source material does not
   65:   support a claim, leave it out. A confidently wrong API signature in an engineering deck
   66:   destroys the credibility of the entire document.
   67: - Match the language of the source material. If it is Korean, write Korean.
   68: 
   69: ## The template you are writing into
   70: 
   71: {{LAYOUT_CATALOG}}
   72: 
   73: ## Output
   74: 
   75: Emit **only** the structured object required by the schema. No prose, no explanation, no
   76: markdown fences around it. Your output is consumed by a program, not read by a human.

    5: >
    6: > This is the only stage that sees what the audience will see. Stages 1 and 2 reason about
    7: > *estimated* text metrics; `fit.py` reasons about *estimated* heights. This stage is the
    8: > ground truth, and it is the only thing that will ever catch a wrong font-metric
    9: > assumption.
   10: >
   11: > Runs **after** the deterministic geometry checks in `fit.detect_overflow_in_png()`.
   12: > Division of labour: arithmetic finds overhangs and collisions far more reliably than a
   13: > vision model does; a vision model finds the things arithmetic cannot name. Do not ask
   14: > this prompt to measure — ask it to judge.
   15: 
   16: ---
   17: 
   18: ## Task
   19: 
   20: You are reviewing one rendered slide from a Vieworks engineering deck. The image attached
   21: is exactly what the audience will see, projected.
   22: 
   23: ## What the slide was supposed to contain
   24: 
   25: ```json
   26: {{SLIDE_IR}}
   27: ```
   28: 
   29: ## Geometry defects already found automatically
   30: 
   31: {{DETERMINISTIC_FINDINGS}}
   32: 
   33: Take these as given — confirm them, do not re-derive them, and do not waste your judgement
   34: re-measuring what has already been measured.
   35: 
   36: ## What to judge
   37: 
   38: Look for the failures that only a viewer can see:
   39: 
   40: 1. **Truncation and overflow.** Text running off any edge, clipped mid-word, or colliding
   41:    with the template's own furniture (logo bar, footer, page number). Note that overflow
   42:    can be *invisible* — text rendered off the bottom simply is not there. If the IR lists
   43:    five bullets and you can count four, say so.
   44: 2. **Legibility at range.** This is projected in a meeting room. Anything under ~12pt
   45:    effective size, low-contrast text on a coloured fill, or a diagram whose edge labels
   46:    have collapsed into each other, fails.
   47: 3. **Density.** Does the slide read in under 15 seconds? A slide that is technically
   48:    correct and visually unreadable is a failed slide.
   49: 4. **Diagram integrity.** Overlapping nodes, crossing edges that obscure labels, arrows
   50:    pointing at nothing, a rasterised diagram gone soft or pixelated.
   51: 5. **Image handling.** Stretched or squashed aspect ratio (people and hardware look
   52:    *wrong* and it is immediately obvious), or an image cropping something important.
   53: 6. **Brand consistency.** Does it look like it belongs to the same deck as the others?
   54:    Off-template colours, an accent that is not the Vieworks red, a stray white box on a
   55:    coloured master.
   56: 
   57: ## What NOT to flag
   58: 
   59: - Wording, tone or content accuracy — not your job here, and you cannot see the sources.
   60: - Aesthetic preferences about the template itself. The master is owned by a designer and
   61:   is not up for review.
   62: - Anything you are merely *unsure* about. A false positive costs a pointless regeneration
   63:   cycle. **When in doubt, pass it.**
   64: 
   65: ## Output
   66: 
   67: ```json
   68: {
   69:   "verdict": "pass" | "fix" | "regenerate",
   70:   "defects": [
   71:     {
   72:       "severity": "critical" | "major" | "minor",
   73:       "kind": "overflow" | "legibility" | "density" | "diagram" | "image" | "brand",
   74:       "description": "<what is wrong, and where on the slide>",
   75:       "suggested_fix": "<a concrete, actionable change — 'cut bullets 4 and 5 and move them to a continuation slide', not 'make it shorter'>"
   76:     }
   77:   ]
   78: }
   79: ```
   80: 
   81: Verdict rules:
   82: 
   83: - **`pass`** — ships as-is. Minor defects alone still pass; perfection is not the bar.
   84: - **`fix`** — a deterministic repair will do it: shrink, split, re-fit. No new content
   85:   needed. `fit.py` handles it, no model call required.
   86: - **`regenerate`** — the slide's *content* is the problem: too much of it, or the wrong
   87:   block type for it. Only this verdict costs another generation call, so reserve it for
   88:   when one is genuinely required.

    1: #!/usr/bin/env python3
    2: # -*- coding: utf-8 -*-
    3: """Slide IR  ->  .pptx, built on the template's own master.
    4: 
    5: STATUS: specification only. Every function below raises NotImplementedError.
    6: 
    7: The rule that makes this whole approach work
    8: [REDACTED]
    9:     prs = Presentation(TEMPLATE_PATH)          # open the REAL template
   10:     layout = prs.slide_layouts[...]            # use ITS layouts
   11:     slide = prs.slides.add_slide(layout)       # inherit master, theme, fonts, brand
   12: 
   13: We never construct ``Presentation()`` empty and restyle it. A blank Presentation carries
   14: python-pptx's stock 4:3 Office theme; every colour and font would have to be re-applied by
   15: hand, the corporate master would be gone, and the output would be un-editable by a designer
   16: in any meaningful way. Opening the template is not an optimisation, it is the design.
   17: 
   18: Corollary: the template ships with N existing slides. ``add_slide`` appends after them, so
   19: the renderer must decide -- and document -- whether it strips them first. Default: strip,
   20: via :func:`clear_existing_slides`, because those slides are the template's *examples*, not
   21: our content.
   22: 
   23: python-pptx gotchas this module has to absorb (details in foundation_research.md S4)
   24: [REDACTED]
   25: 1. **No autofit.** ``text_frame.auto_size`` and ``word_wrap`` set the XML flags, but the
   26:    actual shrink-to-fit is computed by PowerPoint's layout engine at open time -- which
   27:    python-pptx does not have. Text overflows silently. Hence :mod:`aippt.render.fit`.
   28: 2. **Unused placeholders still render.** A layout placeholder that is never filled shows
   29:    its prompt text ("Click to add text") in the *editor*; empty ones can also leave stray
   30:    bullets. Any placeholder not used by a slide MUST be explicitly removed:
   31:    ``ph._element.getparent().remove(ph._element)``.
   32: 3. **No SmartArt.** There is no API and no plausible way to synthesise one. See diagrams.py.
   33: 4. **No native chart-from-image.** Charts must be built via ``add_chart`` with real data,
   34:    which the IR does not currently carry. Out of scope for Phase 1.
   35: 5. **Placeholder geometry may be inherited** (``ph.left`` is None on the layout). The
   36:    manifest already resolves this against the master -- read the manifest, do not re-derive.
   37: """

   56:     unfilled_placeholders_removed: int = 0

   58:

   60:                 manifest: dict[str, Any],

   62:     """Render an IR deck onto the template.

   64:     Args:
   65:         ir: validated IR. Assume ``validate_against_manifest`` has already passed --
   66:             the renderer is not the place to discover that a ``layout_id`` is bogus.
   67:         template: path to ``Vieworks_R&D_ppt_Template.pptx``. Opened, never written.
   68:         out_path: the .pptx to write. Parent dirs created.
   69:         manifest: parsed ``layout_manifest.json``; supplies geometry and budgets.
   70:         strip_existing: remove the template's own example slides first.
   71: 
   72:     Returns:
   73:         A :class:`RenderReport`. A non-empty ``dropped`` list means the deck is WRONG,
   74:         not merely ugly -- the caller should treat it as a failure, not a warning.
   75: 
   76:     Invariants:
   77:         * The template file on disk is byte-identical afterwards.
   78:         * ``len(prs.slides) == len(ir.slides)`` when ``strip_existing`` is True.
   79:         * Slide order matches IR order exactly.
   80:         * No placeholder is left unfilled *and* present (gotcha 2).
   81:     """
   82:     raise NotImplementedError

   84: 
   85: def clear_existing_slides(prs: Any) -> int:
   86:     """Drop the template's example slides, leaving masters and layouts untouched.
   87: 
   88:     python-pptx has no ``slides.remove``. The supported route is through the XML::
   89: 
   90:         xml_slides = prs.slides._sldIdLst
   91:         for sld in list(xml_slides):
   92:             rid = sld.get(qn('r:id'))
   93:             prs.part.drop_rel(rid)          # release the relationship...
   94:             xml_slides.remove(sld)          # ...then unlink the entry
   95: 
   96:     Dropping the ``sldIdLst`` entry WITHOUT ``drop_rel`` leaves an orphaned relationship
   97:     and PowerPoint will report the file as corrupt. Both halves, in that order.
   98: 
   99:     Returns: number of slides removed.
  100:     """
  101:     raise NotImplementedError

  132: 
  133:     Mapping:
  134:         ``b``    -> ``run.font.bold = True``

  136:         ``code`` -> ``run.font.name = 'Consolas'`` + the brand accent colour

  139:     Only ``code`` deviates from the template's inherited styling, and it must: 111 inline

  149:     formatting, so it cannot be used here. Build paragraphs and runs explicitly.

  151:     raise NotImplementedError

  154: def place_block(slide: Any, block: Block, target: Any, budget: dict[str, Any],
  155:                 report: RenderReport) -> None:
  156:     """Render one block into a target placeholder (or a free-floating shape).
  157: 
  158:     Dispatch:
  159:         heading/paragraph/bullets -> paragraphs in the body placeholder, ``para.level``
  160:                                      carrying the bullet level
  161:         callout                   -> rounded rectangle, left accent bar; fill colour by
  162:                                      variant (info/warn/ok)
  163:         table                     -> ``shapes.add_table``; a TABLE placeholder cannot be
  164:                                      filled directly, so add the table at the placeholder's
  165:                                      geometry and then delete the placeholder
  166:         code                      -> monospace textbox on a dark fill. Multi-variant
  167:                                      (the tabbed C++/C# case): PowerPoint has no tabs, so
  168:                                      lay the variants side by side if 2 and they fit,
  169:                                      otherwise emit consecutive slides
  170:         image                     -> :func:`place_image`
  171:         diagram                   -> delegate to :mod:`aippt.render.diagrams`
  172:         steps                     -> numbered chevrons, left to right
  173:         cards                     -> N rounded rectangles across the placeholder width
  174: 
  175:     ``budget`` is this placeholder's entry from the manifest. Check the block against it
  176:     BEFORE writing, and record an overflow warning rather than truncating silently.
  177:     """
  178:     raise NotImplementedError
  179: 
  180: 
  181: def place_image(slide: Any, image_block: Any, target_box: tuple[int, int, int, int]) -> Any:
  182:     """base64 data-URI -> ``add_picture``, aspect-fit inside ``target_box``.
  183: 
  184:     The pipeline (all three corpus images are ``image/jpeg``, not png)::
  185: 
  186:         header, b64 = data_uri.split(",", 1)          # 'data:image/jpeg;base64'
  187:         blob = base64.b64decode(b64)
  188:         stream = io.BytesIO(blob)                      # add_picture accepts a file-like
  189:         pic = slide.shapes.add_picture(stream, left, top, width=..., height=...)
  190: 
  191:     Aspect-fit, not stretch. Passing BOTH width and height to ``add_picture`` distorts the
  192:     image; pass ONE and let python-pptx derive the other from the intrinsic size, then
  193:     centre it in the box::
  194: 
  195:         box_ar = box_w / box_h
  196:         img_ar = pic.image.size[0] / pic.image.size[1]
  197:         if img_ar > box_ar:  scale to width   else:  scale to height
  198: 
  199:     ``pic.image.size`` needs Pillow. Without it python-pptx can still place the picture but
  200:     cannot read its intrinsic dimensions, so aspect-fit silently becomes stretch -- which
  201:     is why Pillow is a hard dependency, not an optional one.
  202: 
  203:     Returns the created picture shape.
  204:     """
  205:     raise NotImplementedError

 succeeded in 690ms:
    1: #!/usr/bin/env python3
    2: # -*- coding: utf-8 -*-
    3: """Text fitting and overflow control. **The #1 design risk of this project.**
    4: 
    5: STATUS: specification only. Every function below raises NotImplementedError.
    6: 
    7: Why this module is the hard one
    8: -------------------------------
    9: PowerPoint's "shrink text on overflow" is not stored as a result, it is stored as an
   10: *instruction* (``<a:normAutofit fontScale="..."/>``) that PowerPoint's layout engine
   11: evaluates when it opens the file. python-pptx writes the instruction but cannot evaluate
   12: it: it has no font metrics, no line breaker, no layout engine. So:
   13: 
   14:     text_frame.word_wrap = True
   15:     text_frame.auto_size = MSO_AUTO_SIZE.TEXT_TO_FIT_SHAPE     # <- does NOT fit the text
   16: 
   17: Overflowing text does not error, does not warn, and does not visibly clip in the XML. It
   18: simply renders off the bottom of the box. **Every deck this project generates is one
   19: verbose LLM sentence away from a broken slide**, and nothing in the stack will tell you.
   20: 
   21: The three-layer defence
   22: -----------------------
   23: Layer 1 -- PREVENT (the only layer that actually scales).
   24:     Inject the per-placeholder content budget into the author prompt. A model told
   25:     "max 6 bullets x 9 words, title <= 42 Korean characters" mostly complies. This is
   26:     cheap, it is upstream of everything, and it removes the problem rather than managing
   27:     it. ``analyze_pptx_master.py`` computes these budgets; that is why it exists.
   28: 
   29: Layer 2 -- MEASURE + DEGRADE (this module).
   30:     Before writing, estimate the rendered height. If it exceeds the box, degrade in a
   31:     fixed, documented order (:func:`fit_text_frame`). Deterministic, no LLM in the loop.
   32: 
   33: Layer 3 -- VERIFY (render -> PNG -> check).
   34:     Layers 1 and 2 both rest on an *estimate*. The only ground truth is a render.
   35:     LibreOffice converts the .pptx to PNG; :func:`detect_overflow_in_png` checks whether
   36:     ink appears where it should not. This is the authoritative check and the only one
   37:     that catches a bad font-metric assumption.
   38: 
   39: A note on honesty: :func:`estimate_text_height` is a model, not a measurement. It will be
   40: wrong sometimes. That is precisely why layer 3 exists and why the degradation ladder is
   41: ordered to fail *safe* (smaller, then fewer) rather than to fail *pretty*.
   42: """
   43: 
   44: from __future__ import annotations
   45: 
   46: from dataclasses import dataclass
   47: from pathlib import Path
   48: from typing import Any
   49: 
   50: #: Must stay in lockstep with the constants in ``analyze_pptx_master.py`` -- if the two
   51: #: models disagree, the budget the LLM was given is not the budget the renderer enforces,
   52: #: and the whole prevent-layer silently stops working. Import them; do not re-type them.
   53: from ..analyze_pptx_master import (  # noqa: F401
   54:     CJK_CHAR_WIDTH_RATIO,
   55:     EMU_PER_PT,
   56:     LATIN_CHAR_WIDTH_RATIO,
   57:     LINE_HEIGHT_RATIO,
   58: )
   59: 
   60: #: Never shrink below this: an 11pt bullet on a projected slide is unreadable from the
   61: #: back of a room, and a legible slide with less content beats a full slide nobody can read.
   62: MIN_BODY_PT = 12.0
   63: MIN_TITLE_PT = 20.0
   64: 
   65: #: How far we will shrink before switching to a different degradation strategy.
   66: MAX_SHRINK_STEPS = 3
   67: SHRINK_STEP_PT = 2.0
   68: 
   69: 
   70: @dataclass
   71: class FitResult:
   72:     fits: bool
   73:     applied_pt: float           # the size actually used
   74:     estimated_lines: int
   75:     overflow_ratio: float       # estimated_height / box_height; > 1.0 means it spills
   76:     action: str                 # "none" | "shrunk" | "split" | "truncated" | "dropped"
   77:     detail: str = ""
   78: 
   79: 
   80: def is_cjk(ch: str) -> bool:
   81:     """True for Hangul / Han / Kana -- the glyphs that are full-width.
   82: 
   83:     Ranges: Hangul syllables U+AC00-D7AF, Hangul Jamo U+1100-11FF, CJK Unified
   84:     U+4E00-9FFF, CJK Ext-A U+3400-4DBF, Kana U+3040-30FF, CJK punctuation U+3000-303F.
   85: 
   86:     This matters more than it looks: the corpus is Korean, and a width model that assumes
   87:     Latin metrics underestimates a Korean line by ~2x. That single mistake would make
   88:     every budget in the catalog wrong in the dangerous direction.
   89:     """
   90:     raise NotImplementedError
   91: 
   92: 
   93: def visual_width(text: str, font_pt: float) -> float:
   94:     """Rendered width of ``text`` in points, mixing scripts correctly.
   95: 
   96:         sum(font_pt * (CJK_CHAR_WIDTH_RATIO if is_cjk(c) else LATIN_CHAR_WIDTH_RATIO)
   97:             for c in text)
   98: 
   99:     A per-character sum, not ``len(text) * ratio``: the corpus routinely mixes
  100:     ("VEO_Focus_SetPosition() 호출 시 모터가 이동합니다") and a single ratio for such a
  101:     string is wrong for both halves.
  102: 
  103:     Bold text is ~5% wider; inline ``code`` is monospace and typically *narrower* per
  104:     char than proportional Latin. Both are inside the SAFETY margin -- do not model them
  105:     until a real render says you must.
  106:     """
  107:     raise NotImplementedError
  108: 
  109: 
  110: def estimate_text_height(text: str, font_pt: float, box_width_emu: int,
  111:                          *, line_spacing: float = LINE_HEIGHT_RATIO) -> tuple[float, int]:
  112:     """Estimate rendered height in points, and the line count, with greedy word wrap.
  113: 
  114:     Wrap on spaces for Latin. **Korean wraps at any character** -- there are no spaces
  115:     inside a Hangul clause -- so a Latin-style word-wrap model badly overestimates the
  116:     line count for Korean text. Break CJK runs per character.
  117: 
  118:     Returns ``(height_pt, line_count)``.
  119:     """
  120:     raise NotImplementedError
  121: 
  122: 
  123: def check_budget(text: str, budget: dict[str, Any], language: str = "mixed") -> FitResult:
  124:     """Check content against a manifest content-budget entry, before writing anything.
  125: 
  126:     ``language`` selects which character budget applies. ``"mixed"`` uses the CJK
  127:     (pessimistic) figure -- if a string contains any CJK at all, budgeting it as Latin
  128:     will overflow.
  129: 
  130:     Cheap and pure. Call it in the AI adapter's validation step so a budget violation is
  131:     caught while a *retry* is still possible, not after the deck is built.
  132:     """
  133:     raise NotImplementedError
  134: 
  135: 
  136: def fit_text_frame(text_frame: Any, blocks: list[Any], box_emu: tuple[int, int],
  137:                    base_pt: float, *, min_pt: float = MIN_BODY_PT) -> FitResult:
  138:     """Make content fit a box. The degradation ladder, in strict order.
  139: 
  140:     1. **Fits as-is** -> write it. Done.
  141:     2. **Shrink** -- reduce font size in ``SHRINK_STEP_PT`` steps, at most
  142:        ``MAX_SHRINK_STEPS``, never below ``min_pt``. Also set ``<a:normAutofit
  143:        fontScale>`` so that PowerPoint agrees with us when a human later edits the text.
  144:     3. **Tighten** -- reduce ``space_before``/``space_after`` and line spacing to 0.9.
  145:        Buys roughly one line. Visually cheap.
  146:     4. **Split** -- move the overflow onto a continuation slide using the same layout,
  147:        title suffixed " (cont.)". This is the *correct* answer for genuinely long content
  148:        and the one a human would choose.
  149:     5. **Truncate** -- last resort, and it is a defect: record it in ``RenderReport.dropped``
  150:        and let the caller decide whether to fail the build. Never truncate quietly.
  151: 
  152:     Deliberately NOT in the ladder: rewriting the text with an LLM to make it shorter.
  153:     That is layer 1's job, upstream, where the model still has the source material and the
  154:     intent. Doing it here -- with only the rendered string in hand -- produces a summary
  155:     of a summary, and the result reads like it.
  156: 
  157:     Returns the :class:`FitResult` describing what was actually done.
  158:     """
  159:     raise NotImplementedError
  160: 
  161: 
  162: def split_overflow(blocks: list[Any], box_emu: tuple[int, int], font_pt: float) -> tuple[list[Any], list[Any]]:
  163:     """Split blocks into ``(fits_on_this_slide, goes_to_continuation)``.
  164: 
  165:     Split at block boundaries where possible; split *within* a bullets block only if a
  166:     single bullets block alone overflows. Never split a table's header from its body, and
  167:     never orphan a heading from the first bullet under it -- both look like a rendering
  168:     bug rather than a deliberate break.
  169:     """
  170:     raise NotImplementedError
  171: 
  172: 
  173: def detect_overflow_in_png(png_path: Path, manifest_layout: dict[str, Any],
  174:                            *, tolerance_px: int = 4) -> list[str]:
  175:     """Layer 3: the authoritative check. Look at the rendered slide.
  176: 
  177:     Render with::
  178: 
  179:         soffice --headless --convert-to pdf deck.pptx     # then pdftoppm -png
  180:         # (LibreOffice's direct --convert-to png only renders the first slide)
  181: 
  182:     Then, per slide, detect:
  183:       * ink within ``tolerance_px`` of any slide edge -> content ran off the canvas;
  184:       * a text placeholder whose ink bbox is taller than its declared box -> overflow;
  185:       * overlapping ink bboxes from two different placeholders -> collision.
  186: 
  187:     Returns a list of human-readable defects, empty if clean. These strings feed straight
  188:     into ``prompts/30_visual_qa.md`` as the deterministic half of the QA loop -- the vision
  189:     model handles the aesthetic half ("this diagram is unreadable"), but geometry is better
  190:     checked with arithmetic than with a vision model, which will confidently miss a 3-pixel
  191:     overhang.
  192:     """
  193:     raise NotImplementedError

    1: # Foundation Research — AI-Generated PowerPoint on the Vieworks R&D Template
    2: 
    3: **Status:** framework + research. Not an implementation.
    4: **Date:** 2026-07-13
    5: 
    6: ---
    7: 
    8: ## ⚠️ Epistemic status — what is verified and what is not
    9: 
   10: This document distinguishes three grades of claim, and marks them, because the difference
   11: matters enormously to anyone acting on it.
   12: 
   13: | Grade | Meaning |
   14: |---|---|
   15: | ✅ **VERIFIED** | Measured directly from the source files in this session. |
   16: | 📖 **KNOWN** | Documented behaviour of python-pptx / OOXML. Confident, but not re-tested here. |
   17: | ❌ **UNVERIFIED** | **Depends on opening the `.pptx`, which did not happen.** Treat as an open question. |
   18: 
   19: **The PoC was never run.** `analyze_pptx_master.py` is written and complete, but the
   20: session that produced it had no code-execution permission — Python was denied through the
   21: shell, through PowerShell, and through a subagent. Consequently *nothing* about the
   22: template's internals is confirmed: not the layout count, not the layout names, not the box
   23: geometry, not the font sizes, and not where `#DB0032` lives.
   24: 
   25: Every such gap below is marked ❌ and left empty. It would have taken thirty seconds to
   26: fill them with plausible invented numbers, and that would have been the single most
   27: damaging thing this document could do: the layout catalog is injected verbatim into every
   28: AI prompt, so a fabricated one propagates into every generated deck and fails far
   29: downstream, wearing the disguise of a renderer bug.
   30: 
   31: **Run `python -m aippt.analyze_pptx_master` and fill in §4.** Everything else here holds
   32: regardless of what it prints.
   33: 
   34: ---
   35: 
   36: ## 1. Problem, goals, and the three approaches we reject
   37: 
   38: ### The problem
   39: 
   40: Vieworks R&D produces technical decks — SDK guides, architecture proposals, test-bench
   41: designs. Today they are authored as hand-built HTML "card" decks (30 slides across three
   42: files, see §3). They look good in a browser, and they are not PowerPoint: they cannot be
   43: handed to a colleague to edit, they do not carry the corporate master, and they cannot be
   44: dropped into a customer-facing deck.
   45: 
   46: Meanwhile `Vieworks_R&D_ppt_Template.pptx` — the real corporate template, with the real
   47: master, the real fonts and the real brand red — sits unused by this workflow.
   48: 
   49: ### Goals
   50: 
   51: 1. Generate `.pptx` decks that are **indistinguishable from human-authored ones built on
   52:    the corporate template** — because they *are* built on it.
   53: 2. Output must be **fully editable**: real text in real placeholders, real shapes. A
   54:    designer must be able to open it and move things.
   55: 3. **Two front doors, one engine:** convert the existing HTML decks, *and* author new decks
   56:    from a topic + source material.
   57: 4. **Model-agnostic.** Claude, Codex and Gemini are swappable authors behind one interface.
   58: 
   59: ### Rejected: (a) headless-browser screenshot → image slides
   60: 
   61: Render the HTML in Chrome, screenshot each card, `add_picture` it onto a blank slide.
   62: 
   63: Seductive because it is a weekend's work and the output looks *pixel-identical* to the
   64: HTML. Rejected because it produces a deck of **pictures of slides**:
   65: 
   66: - Nothing is editable. Not one word. Fixing a typo means re-rendering the source HTML —
   67:   which most of the audience for these decks does not have and could not run.
   68: - The corporate master is bypassed entirely. The output inherits the *browser's*
   69:   typography, not the template's. It only coincidentally resembles a Vieworks deck.
   70: - No text layer: no search, no screen reader, no copy-paste, no reflow.
   71: - It hard-codes the current HTML CSS as the de facto design system, permanently. The
   72:   designer who owns the master has been written out of the loop.
   73: 
   74: This approach optimises for the demo and sacrifices the product. It is the one to be most
   75: careful about, precisely because the first screenshot looks so convincing.
   76: 
   77: ### Rejected: (b) the LLM writes DrawingML / OOXML directly
   78: 
   79: Ask the model for `<p:sp>` elements.
   80: 
   81: - OOXML is verbose, deeply namespaced, and unforgiving. A single misplaced element yields
   82:   "PowerPoint found unreadable content" — with no indication of which of 4,000 lines is at
   83:   fault.
   84: - The failure mode is *binary and opaque*. There is no partial credit and no useful error,
   85:   so the repair loop has nothing to work with.
   86: - It burns an enormous number of tokens on syntax that a template already encodes perfectly.
   87: - It puts geometry in the hands of the component with the least reliable grasp of it.
   88:   Models are excellent at prose and structure; they are not a layout engine.
   89: 
   90: ### Rejected: (c) build from a blank `Presentation()`
   91: 
   92: Create an empty deck and restyle it to match the brand.
   93: 
   94: - `Presentation()` with no argument loads python-pptx's **stock 4:3 Office template**. Not
   95:   the Vieworks master. 📖
   96: - Every colour, font, size and position would have to be re-applied by hand — a
   97:   reimplementation of the template in Python, which must then be kept in sync with a
   98:   `.pptx` that a designer will keep editing. It will drift, and the drift will be silent.
   99: - The master, the layouts and the theme are *gone*. A user who opens the deck and picks
  100:   "New Slide" gets nothing that matches.
  101: 
  102: The template is not a style guide to be imitated. It is the source of truth, and the
  103: correct move is to **open it and add slides to it**.
  104: 
  105: ### What we do instead
  106: 
  107: Open the real template. Call `add_slide()` with the template's **own** `slide_layouts`. Put
  108: text in placeholders. Let the master do what a master is for.
  109: 
  110: ---
  111: 
  112: ## 2. The pipeline
  113: 
  114: ```
  115:                           reference/layout_manifest.json
  116:                           reference/layout_catalog.md
  117:                                     │  (generated once by analyze_pptx_master.py)
  118:                                     │
  119:                                     ▼
  120:   HTML deck ──> html_adapter ──┐         ┌──> layout_id + content budgets
  121:                                ├─> [ Slide IR ] ─> renderer ─> deck.pptx
  122:   topic + sources ─> ai_adapter┘     (JSON,          │
  123:                           ▲           schema-        │
  124:                           │           validated)     ▼
  125:                           │                    LibreOffice ─> PDF ─> PNG
  126:                           │                          │
  127:                           │                          ▼
  128:                           │                 geometry checks (fit.py)
  129:                           │                          +
  130:                           └───── repair ◄──── vision critique (30_visual_qa.md)
  131: ```
  132: 
  133: Four properties this shape buys, none of which are free:
  134: 
  135: **The IR is the contract.** Adapters and renderer are coupled only through
  136: `ir/schema.json`. A new input source (Markdown, Confluence, a Word doc) is a new adapter
  137: and nothing else changes. A new output target is a new renderer.
  138: 
  139: **The AI is confined to one box.** It writes `layout_id` + content. It cannot emit
  140: geometry because the schema has nowhere to put geometry. The invariant is enforced by the
  141: data model, not by asking the model nicely — which is the only kind of enforcement that
  142: survives contact with a model that is having an off day.
  143: 
  144: **Determinism where determinism is possible.** Layout, geometry and styling are code.
  145: Only *content* is probabilistic. When a deck comes out wrong, the question "is this a
  146: model problem or a code problem?" has a crisp answer.
  147: 
  148: **Model-agnosticism is structural, not aspirational.** All three providers can constrain
  149: output to a JSON Schema. The schema is the neutral contract; the provider is an
  150: implementation detail behind `LLMClient`.
  151: 
  152: ---
  153: 
  154: ## 3. The Slide IR — and how the corpus reshaped it ✅
  155: 
  156: The full annotated schema is `src/aippt/ir/schema.json`; the typed mirror is
  157: `src/aippt/ir/models.py`. This section explains *why* it has the shape it has.
  158: 
  159: ### The corpus is the spec
  160: 
  161: The block taxonomy was not designed in the abstract. It was **counted**, directly, out of
  162: the three existing decks:
  163: 
  164: | Construct | Occurrences | What it forced |
  165: |---|---:|---|
  166: | `<section class="card">` | **30** | one card = one slide; the whole IR hangs off this |
  167: | `<h2>` | **30** | exactly one title per card — an invariant, not a convention |
  168: | inline `<code>` | **111** | `rich_text` runs are **mandatory** |
  169: | `<h3>` | 28 | `heading` is a first-class block |
  170: | `<ul>` | 22 | `bullets`, with levels |
  171: | `<table>` | 11 | `table` — and this is the block most likely to overflow |
  172: | `.step-badge` | 10 | `steps` |
  173: | `.mini-card` | 9 | `cards` (in `.grid-2` / `.grid-3`) |
  174: | `.tag` | 8 | a field on `cards` |
  175: | `<svg>` | 7 | hand-drawn diagrams — the mermaid path will **not** catch these |
  176: | `.alert-box` | 4 | `callout`, variant `info` |
  177: | base64 `<img>` | **3** | all `image/jpeg` |
  178: | `.mermaid` | 3 | `diagram` |
  179: | `.warn-box` | 2 | `callout`, variant `warn` |
  180: | `<pre>` | 2 | `code` |
